I mean, I worry about normal things—work, bills, whether I’m doing things right—but I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or about everything. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after a particular stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, yes. I wouldn’t call it a huge stressful event exactly, but it’s been about a month. My husband stopped working outside the home, and our usual routine changed. I think that’s around when I started noticing the focus problem more. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have off days like anybody, but I’m still getting through things. It’s more… I don’t know, feeling a little worn out sometimes. But not really down in a serious way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down to do them. Maybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more stressed since your husband stopped working outside the home?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been some terrible thing, but since he stopped working outside the home and our routine changed, I’ve felt more thrown off. It’s been about a month now, I think. Is that… is that the kind of stress you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you been feeling unusually distressed about that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unusually distressed. I mean, it’s been an adjustment, and I’ve felt a bit unsettled by the change in routine, but I don’t want to overstate it. I think I’m handling it okay… or at least trying to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of unsure how things are going, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Are these focus problems upsetting you a lot?\n\nPatient: A lot… I don’t know if I’d say a lot. It’s upsetting, yes. At work I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made some inventory mistakes, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like to admit. It kind of feels like we’re walking around each other instead of settling into him being retired. I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Since your routine changed, have you been feeling keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after my shifts I’m not as wiped out as I usually would be. I come home kind of… buzzing, and instead of sitting on the couch, I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage. It feels like relaxing should be easy, but sitting still just feels kind of impossible sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything. I don’t think I’m going without sleep on purpose… maybe some nights feel shorter, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I mean, I’m sure I got distracted sometimes like anyone, but what’s bothering me now is more at work—like in the warehouse, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, I lose the count I was doing and then later realize I entered the wrong numbers. It just feels more noticeable now, if that answers your question.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, bills, whether I’m doing things right—but I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or about everything. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after a particular stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, yes. I wouldn’t call it a huge stressful event exactly, but it’s been about a month. My husband stopped working outside the home, and our usual routine changed. I think that’s around when I started noticing the focus problem more. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have off days like anybody, but I’m still getting through things. It’s more… I don’t know, feeling a little worn out sometimes. But not really down in a serious way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down to do them. Maybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more stressed since your husband stopped working outside the home?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been some terrible thing, but since he stopped working outside the home and our routine changed, I’ve felt more thrown off. It’s been about a month now, I think. Is that… is that the kind of stress you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you been feeling unusually distressed about that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unusually distressed. I mean, it’s been an adjustment, and I’ve felt a bit unsettled by the change in routine, but I don’t want to overstate it. I think I’m handling it okay… or at least trying to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of unsure how things are going, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Are these focus problems upsetting you a lot?\n\nPatient: A lot… I don’t know if I’d say a lot. It’s upsetting, yes. At work I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made some inventory mistakes, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like to admit. It kind of feels like we’re walking around each other instead of settling into him being retired. I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Since your routine changed, have you been feeling keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after my shifts I’m not as wiped out as I usually would be. I come home kind of… buzzing, and instead of sitting on the couch, I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage. It feels like relaxing should be easy, but sitting still just feels kind of impossible sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything. I don’t think I’m going without sleep on purpose… maybe some nights feel shorter, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energetic or unusually up lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. Some days I feel almost… too upbeat, I guess? Like I suddenly think I can get the whole house straightened out and fix things at work before dinner, which sounds good, but then if my husband questions one of my plans, I get irritated and snap over something small. I’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as “unusually up,” but it’s different for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.81, F30.8, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally pushed me to come in is I’m having trouble keeping my mind on one thing, especially at work. I’m in the warehouse, and if a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or even someone moving a pallet nearby, I get pulled right off what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless, but I’ll be doing a count and then realize I put the wrong numbers into inventory. It’s embarrassing. I just figured I should probably talk to someone before it becomes a bigger problem… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble staying focused when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember it being like this when I was a kid. I mean, I’m sure I got distracted sometimes like anyone, but what’s bothering me now is more at work—like in the warehouse, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, I lose the count I was doing and then later realize I entered the wrong numbers. It just feels more noticeable now, if that answers your question.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, bills, whether I’m doing things right—but I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or about everything. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after a particular stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, yes. I wouldn’t call it a huge stressful event exactly, but it’s been about a month. My husband stopped working outside the home, and our usual routine changed. I think that’s around when I started noticing the focus problem more. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have off days like anybody, but I’m still getting through things. It’s more… I don’t know, feeling a little worn out sometimes. But not really down in a serious way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down to do them. Maybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more stressed since your husband stopped working outside the home?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been some terrible thing, but since he stopped working outside the home and our routine changed, I’ve felt more thrown off. It’s been about a month now, I think. Is that… is that the kind of stress you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you been feeling unusually distressed about that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unusually distressed. I mean, it’s been an adjustment, and I’ve felt a bit unsettled by the change in routine, but I don’t want to overstate it. I think I’m handling it okay… or at least trying to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of unsure how things are going, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Are these focus problems upsetting you a lot?\n\nPatient: A lot… I don’t know if I’d say a lot. It’s upsetting, yes. At work I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made some inventory mistakes, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like to admit. It kind of feels like we’re walking around each other instead of settling into him being retired. I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Since your routine changed, have you been feeling keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after my shifts I’m not as wiped out as I usually would be. I come home kind of… buzzing, and instead of sitting on the couch, I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage. It feels like relaxing should be easy, but sitting still just feels kind of impossible sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything. I don’t think I’m going without sleep on purpose… maybe some nights feel shorter, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energetic or unusually up lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. Some days I feel almost… too upbeat, I guess? Like I suddenly think I can get the whole house straightened out and fix things at work before dinner, which sounds good, but then if my husband questions one of my plans, I get irritated and snap over something small. I’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as “unusually up,” but it’s different for me.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been needing less sleep than usual and still feeling rested?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say I’m feeling rested, exactly. I might be sleeping a bit lighter or getting up earlier some nights, but I haven’t really paid close attention. I don’t want to give you the wrong impression—maybe it’s just my schedule being off. Is that helpful, or do you mean like feeling totally fine on only a few hours?\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been more active than usual in getting things done or starting projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess maybe a bit more than usual. Not in a bad way, I don’t think, but after my shifts I usually want to just sit down, and lately I’ve been coming home still kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage because sitting on the couch feels almost impossible. I don’t know if that’s the kind of “more active” you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I just figured I should probably talk to someone before it becomes a bigger problem… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble staying focused when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember it being like this when I was a kid. I mean, I’m sure I got distracted sometimes like anyone, but what’s bothering me now is more at work—like in the warehouse, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, I lose the count I was doing and then later realize I entered the wrong numbers. It just feels more noticeable now, if that answers your question.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, bills, whether I’m doing things right—but I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or about everything. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after a particular stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, yes. I wouldn’t call it a huge stressful event exactly, but it’s been about a month. My husband stopped working outside the home, and our usual routine changed. I think that’s around when I started noticing the focus problem more. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have off days like anybody, but I’m still getting through things. It’s more… I don’t know, feeling a little worn out sometimes. But not really down in a serious way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down to do them. Maybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more stressed since your husband stopped working outside the home?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been some terrible thing, but since he stopped working outside the home and our routine changed, I’ve felt more thrown off. It’s been about a month now, I think. Is that… is that the kind of stress you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you been feeling unusually distressed about that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unusually distressed. I mean, it’s been an adjustment, and I’ve felt a bit unsettled by the change in routine, but I don’t want to overstate it. I think I’m handling it okay… or at least trying to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of unsure how things are going, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Are these focus problems upsetting you a lot?\n\nPatient: A lot… I don’t know if I’d say a lot. It’s upsetting, yes. At work I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made some inventory mistakes, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like to admit. It kind of feels like we’re walking around each other instead of settling into him being retired. I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Since your routine changed, have you been feeling keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after my shifts I’m not as wiped out as I usually would be. I come home kind of… buzzing, and instead of sitting on the couch, I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage. It feels like relaxing should be easy, but sitting still just feels kind of impossible sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything. I don’t think I’m going without sleep on purpose… maybe some nights feel shorter, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energetic or unusually up lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. Some days I feel almost… too upbeat, I guess? Like I suddenly think I can get the whole house straightened out and fix things at work before dinner, which sounds good, but then if my husband questions one of my plans, I get irritated and snap over something small. I’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as “unusually up,” but it’s different for me.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been needing less sleep than usual and still feeling rested?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say I’m feeling rested, exactly. I might be sleeping a bit lighter or getting up earlier some nights, but I haven’t really paid close attention. I don’t want to give you the wrong impression—maybe it’s just my schedule being off. Is that helpful, or do you mean like feeling totally fine on only a few hours?\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been more active than usual in getting things done or starting projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess maybe a bit more than usual. Not in a bad way, I don’t think, but after my shifts I usually want to just sit down, and lately I’ve been coming home still kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage because sitting on the couch feels almost impossible. I don’t know if that’s the kind of “more active” you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 4 days in a row lately when you felt more energized or more up than usual?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… probably more than 4 days in a row. It’s been kind of on and off for about a month now, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s about when I noticed I was more keyed up than usual.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you felt unusually confident in yourself or your abilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unusually confident. Is that the kind of confidence you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these more energized periods, have your symptoms caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Not serious serious, I don’t think… but there have been a couple things. I mean, I’m sure I got distracted sometimes like anyone, but what’s bothering me now is more at work—like in the warehouse, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, I lose the count I was doing and then later realize I entered the wrong numbers. It just feels more noticeable now, if that answers your question.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, bills, whether I’m doing things right—but I wouldn’t say it’s all the time or about everything. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after a particular stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, yes. I wouldn’t call it a huge stressful event exactly, but it’s been about a month. My husband stopped working outside the home, and our usual routine changed. I think that’s around when I started noticing the focus problem more. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have off days like anybody, but I’m still getting through things. It’s more… I don’t know, feeling a little worn out sometimes. But not really down in a serious way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down to do them. Maybe I’m a little distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more stressed since your husband stopped working outside the home?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s been some terrible thing, but since he stopped working outside the home and our routine changed, I’ve felt more thrown off. It’s been about a month now, I think. Is that… is that the kind of stress you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you been feeling unusually distressed about that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unusually distressed. I mean, it’s been an adjustment, and I’ve felt a bit unsettled by the change in routine, but I don’t want to overstate it. I think I’m handling it okay… or at least trying to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of unsure how things are going, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Are these focus problems upsetting you a lot?\n\nPatient: A lot… I don’t know if I’d say a lot. It’s upsetting, yes. At work I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made some inventory mistakes, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like to admit. It kind of feels like we’re walking around each other instead of settling into him being retired. I’m trying not to make too big a deal of it, but… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Since your routine changed, have you been feeling keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after my shifts I’m not as wiped out as I usually would be. I come home kind of… buzzing, and instead of sitting on the couch, I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage. It feels like relaxing should be easy, but sitting still just feels kind of impossible sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything. I don’t think I’m going without sleep on purpose… maybe some nights feel shorter, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energetic or unusually up lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. Some days I feel almost… too upbeat, I guess? Like I suddenly think I can get the whole house straightened out and fix things at work before dinner, which sounds good, but then if my husband questions one of my plans, I get irritated and snap over something small. I’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as “unusually up,” but it’s different for me.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been needing less sleep than usual and still feeling rested?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say I’m feeling rested, exactly. I might be sleeping a bit lighter or getting up earlier some nights, but I haven’t really paid close attention. I don’t want to give you the wrong impression—maybe it’s just my schedule being off. Is that helpful, or do you mean like feeling totally fine on only a few hours?\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been more active than usual in getting things done or starting projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess maybe a bit more than usual. Not in a bad way, I don’t think, but after my shifts I usually want to just sit down, and lately I’ve been coming home still kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage because sitting on the couch feels almost impossible. I don’t know if that’s the kind of “more active” you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 4 days in a row lately when you felt more energized or more up than usual?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… probably more than 4 days in a row. It’s been kind of on and off for about a month now, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s about when I noticed I was more keyed up than usual.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you felt unusually confident in yourself or your abilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unusually confident. I mean, I’ve maybe felt a little more like I can tackle things, but not like I’m suddenly amazing at everything or anything like that. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of confidence you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these more energized periods, have your symptoms caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Not serious serious, I don’t think… but there have been a couple things. At work I’ve made some inventory mistakes and had to go back and double-check with my supervisor, which is embarrassing because I’m usually pretty careful.\n\nAnd at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like. It’s not like anything awful, but it feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of settling into him being home more. I don’t know if that counts as serious problems, but it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: During these more energized times, have you been talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe I have been. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but my husband and a couple people at work have said I’m talking really fast and they can’t really get a word in.\n\nIt’ll be about little things, like recipes I want to try, or safety ideas at work, or these art classes I’ve been thinking about. In my head it feels like I need to get the whole thought out before it disappears, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a little much.\n\nDoctor: During these more energized times, does it feel like your thoughts are moving unusually fast?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “unusually fast,” exactly. Sometimes I feel like I’m trying to keep track of a few things at once, but I don’t know if that’s just me being busy or distracted.\n\nWhat do you mean by moving fast—like jumping from one idea to another? Because I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
